### Tool result

_source line 9 · 2026-05-15T23:11:00.579Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WZkgsjQgbSEtXB3YVfjFUo`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	As large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\to$data$\to$model loop arises---yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.
3	We formalize \emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.
4	From each passage we extract 19 statistical features---perplexity percentiles, surprisal moments, and lexical diversity measures---computed against a fixed reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
5	A Random Forest classifier achieves 70--85\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\%; 58.8--82.6\% when excluding the near-perfectly separable d0 class; a zero-shot LLM baseline reaches only 36.2\%), with 90th-percentile perplexity and surprisal variance emerging as consistent depth markers across the studied $3 \times 3$ grid (top-2 importance in 9/9 and 7/9 cells, respectively).
6	Our \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas} reveals that decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories rather than merely attenuating them, and we define the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for extended recursion chains.
7	Cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\%; temperature sampling induces a d1/d2 AUC inversion that we decompose into a correctable scale component and a residual structural component.
8	Applied to WikiText-103 (546 passages), all three model-specific classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\% domain shift in mean perplexity; on C4 web-crawl text, false positive rates range from 4.7\% to 22.4\%, concentrated in template content.
9	\end{abstract}
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 11 · 2026-05-15T23:11:00.738Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CS5cjZWurhdmbHX8oQeJn4`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	The text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.
5	As synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.
6	This model$\to$data$\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical---it is already occurring in the wild---yet we lack the ability to determine how many recursive iterations a given text has undergone, a distinction that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.
7	
8	Existing tools address adjacent but distinct problems.
9	Binary detectors such as Binoculars \cite{binoculars2024}, Fast-DetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, and DivEye \cite{diveye2026} classify text as human-written or machine-generated, collapsing all depths $d \geq 1$ into a single category.
10	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding and cannot be applied retroactively.
11	The model collapse literature \cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.
12	No existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone.
13	
14	Our key insight is that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are consistent across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.
15	Specifically, perplexity tail statistics---the 90th-percentile perplexity (\textsc{p90\_ppl}) and surprisal variance (\textsc{var\_surprisal})---emerge as dominant depth indicators, ranking in the top two features by importance in 9/9 and 8/9 model--strategy configurations, respectively.
16	
17	We formalize recursive generation depth estimation as an ordinal classification task and introduce a lightweight pipeline to solve it.
18	Given a text passage, we compute a 19-dimensional feature vector from reference-model perplexity statistics---capturing distributional location, spread, tail behavior, and lexical diversity---and classify it with a Random Forest into one of $K{+}1$ depth classes ($d{=}0$ for human text through $d{=}K$ for $K$-th generation text).
19	To systematically characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \times 3$ grid of model families (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and decoding strategies (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, temperature $\tau{=}0.9$, top-$k$ $k{=}50$), each spanning 4 generation depths, totaling 180K samples.
20	The atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories---temperature sampling accelerates convergence toward a low-entropy attractor, while top-$k$ attenuates the depth signal---and we introduce the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.
21	
22	Our contributions are:
23	
24	\begin{itemize}
25	    \item \textbf{Task formalization.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and introduce the EDD metric, which summarizes the maximum depth at which adjacent generations remain separable above a given AUC threshold.
26	
27	    \item \textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} We construct a systematic $3 \times 3$ map across model families and decoding strategies, revealing that \textsc{p90\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in all 9 cells and \textsc{var\_surprisal} in 8/9, with a mean Spearman rank correlation of $\rho{=}0.868$ across importance vectors. The extended Pythia nucleus pipeline (d0--d5) yields $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$, establishing that depth discrimination persists through four recursive steps before falling below threshold.
28	
29	    \item \textbf{Generalization analysis.} Cross-model leave-one-out transfer achieves 66.4\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\% (chance = 25\%). We decompose a temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion into a scale component---partially correctable by per-strategy normalization---and a residual structural component that simple normalization cannot resolve.
30	
31	    \item \textbf{End-to-end learning.} Both DeBERTa-v3-base (58.3\%) and RoBERTa-base (59.5\%) achieve ${\sim}$59\% via end-to-end finetuning---19pp below feature-based classifiers (78.2\%)---with the architecture-invariant gap ruling out model selection bias. Linear probing confirms that PLM representations cannot recover perplexity tail features (negative $R^2$), while SHAP analysis shows these features account for 88.8\% of RF's contribution.
32	\end{itemize}
```

statistics---the 90th-percentile perplexity (\\textsc{p90\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance (\\textsc{var\\_surprisal})---emerge as dominant depth indicators, ranking in the top two features by importance in 9/9 and 8/9 model--strategy configurations, respectively.\n\nWe formalize recursive generation depth estimation as an ordinal classification task and introduce a lightweight pipeline to solve it.\nGiven a text passage, we compute a 19-dimensional feature vector from reference-model perplexity statistics---capturing distributional location, spread, tail behavior, and lexical diversity---and classify it with a Random Forest into one of $K{+}1$ depth classes ($d{=}0$ for human text through $d{=}K$ for $K$-th generation text).\nTo systematically characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and decoding strategies (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, temperature $\\tau{=}0.9$, top-$k$ $k{=}50$), each spanning 4 generation depths, totaling 180K samples.\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories---temperature sampling accelerates convergence toward a low-entropy attractor, while top-$k$ attenuates the depth signal---and we introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and introduce the EDD metric, which summarizes the maximum depth at which adjacent generations remain separable above a given AUC threshold.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} We construct a systematic $3 \\times 3$ map across model families and decoding strategies, revealing that \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in all 9 cells and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9, with a mean Spearman rank correlation of $\\rho{=}0.868$ across importance vectors. The extended Pythia nucleus pipeline (d0--d5) yields $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$, establishing that depth discrimination persists through four recursive steps before falling below threshold.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Generalization analysis.} Cross-model leave-one-out transfer achieves 66.4\\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\\% (chance = 25\\%). We decompose a temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion into a scale component---partially correctable by per-strategy normalization---and a residual structural component that simple normalization cannot resolve.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{End-to-end learning.} Both DeBERTa-v3-base (58.3\\%) and RoBERTa-base (59.5\\%) achieve ${\\sim}$59\\% via end-to-end finetuning---19pp below feature-based classifiers (78.2\\%)---with the architecture-invariant gap ruling out model selection bias. Linear probing confirms that PLM representations cannot recover perplexity tail features (negative $R^2$), while SHAP analysis shows these features account for 88.8\\% of RF's contribution.\n\\end{itemize}",
      "numLines": 32,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 32
    }
  }
}

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Model Collapse Theory.}
5	\citet{shumailov2024_nature} demonstrate that recursive training on model outputs erodes distributional tails, collapsing the output distribution.
6	\citet{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} formalize this via scaling laws distinguishing tail \emph{narrowing} from \emph{cutting}; \citet{dohmatob2024_strong_collapse} establish strong collapse guarantees, and \citet{dohmatob2024_demystified} analyze the regression case.
7	The MAD framework \cite{alemohammad2024_mad} extends this to self-consuming models and quantifies the precision--recall tradeoff.
8	Mitigation strategies include retaining real data \cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate} and filtering via detection \cite{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse}.
9	Recent work identifies non-monotonic dynamics \cite{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement}, fixed-point convergence \cite{marchi2024_heat_death}, contamination bounds \cite{wang2026_contamination}, curated pipelines \cite{falahati2026_curated}, knowledge collapse \cite{knowledge_collapse2025}, spectral signatures \cite{sigma2026_spectral}, and self-improving loops \cite{synthetic_eggs2026}.
10	These works characterize degradation but assume depth is known a priori; we address the inverse problem of estimating depth from text alone.
11	
12	\paragraph{AI Text Detection.}
13	DetectGPT \cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt} introduced probability curvature as a zero-shot detection signal; Fast-DetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt2024} improves efficiency via surrogate-model conditional curvature.
14	Binoculars \cite{binoculars2024} exploits perplexity ratios between related models, DivEye \cite{diveye2026} leverages surprisal diversity, and GLTR \cite{gehrmann2019_gltr} visualizes per-token likelihood ranks.
15	Fundamental limits exist: paraphrasing reduces detection to near chance \cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase}, detection degrades under distribution shift \cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and a generator barrier bounds performance when the generator is unknown \cite{pu2026_generator_barrier}.
16	Ghostbuster \cite{verma2024_ghostbuster} trains classifiers on statistical features for binary detection, sharing our feature-engineering philosophy but not addressing depth; M4-SemEval \cite{wang2024_m4_semeval} benchmarks multilingual detection, and DNA-GPT \cite{yang2024_dnagpt} uses divergent $n$-gram analysis.
17	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} embeds detectable signals but requires proactive deployment.
18	All these methods perform binary classification, discarding ordinal depth information; our approach outputs ordinal depth labels, complementing rather than replacing binary detection.
19	
20	\paragraph{Adjacent Tasks.}
21	EditLens \cite{editlens2026} estimates editing extent but does not model recursive chains.
22	\citet{uchendu2020_attribution} classify \emph{which} model generated text rather than generation depth.
23	LOKI \cite{loki2025} benchmarks synthetic data detection across modalities but evaluates binary detection only.
24	\citet{mohamed2025_broken_telephone} study iterative generation as a ``broken telephone'' effect, measuring forward degradation; our task performs the inverse, classifying depth from text alone.
25	
26	\paragraph{Ordinal Classification.}
27	CORAL \cite{coral2020} enforces rank consistency via ordered binary tasks; CLOC \cite{cloc2025} extends contrastive learning with multi-margin ordinal losses.
28	We adopt both as baselines (\S\ref{sec:generalization}) to confirm the signal is accessible to principled ordinal methods.
29	
30	\paragraph{Decoding Strategy Effects.}
31	Decoding strategies shape text statistics in ways that interact with depth signals: nucleus sampling maintains diversity \cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, decoding parameters affect evaluation non-trivially \cite{finlayson2024_closing}, and strategy effects are model-dependent \cite{garces_arias2025_decoding_decoded}.
32	\citet{wiher2022_decoding} systematically compare methods on surface-level properties, and Mirostat \cite{basu2021_mirostat} regulates output entropy via perplexity control.
33	Our Depth Degradation Atlas (\S\ref{sec:atlas}) reveals that these strategies induce qualitatively different degradation trajectories under recursive generation---temperature sampling accelerates convergence to a low-entropy attractor while top-$k$ attenuates the depth signal---effects beyond single-pass text quality.
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Problem Definition}
5	\label{sec:method:problem}
6	
7	Let $f_\theta$ denote a language model and $\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.
8	We define a \emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\mathcal{D}_0, \mathcal{D}_1, \dots, \mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\theta$ on $\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.
9	Depth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.
10	
11	Given a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \in \{0, 1, \dots, K\}$.
12	This is an ordinal classification problem: depths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.
13	
14	\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}
15	\label{sec:method:benchmark}
16	
17	We construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.
18	
19	\paragraph{Model families.}
20	We select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).
21	Restricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.
22	
23	\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}
24	Each model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).
25	These span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.
26	Our findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.
27	
28	\paragraph{Depth and scale.}
29	The primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).
30	For depth extension experiments (\S\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.
31	
32	\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}
33	Starting from a Wikipedia subset as seed text ($\mathcal{D}_0$), each generation cycle proceeds in three steps: (1)~LoRA finetuning \cite{hu2022lora} on $\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$, freezing base model weights and training low-rank update matrices ($<$1\% additional parameters; $r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$, architecture-specific targets); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages from the LoRA-finetuned model (max 512 tokens, BOS-seeded, no prompt conditioning) under the specified decoding strategy; (3)~the generated corpus becomes $\mathcal{D}_k$ for the next cycle.
34	Each depth is produced by a model explicitly trained on the prior depth's data---not by sampling from the unmodified base model.
35	There is no per-passage lineage across depths, ensuring cross-validation splits do not introduce information leakage.
36	
37	\paragraph{Reference model.}
38	All statistical features (\S\ref{sec:method:features}) are computed using a fixed reference model---GPT-2 base (124M)---that is independent of the three generation models.
39	The reference model computes per-token perplexity for each passage, providing a consistent measurement frame across model families.
40	In cross-model leave-one-out evaluation (\S\ref{sec:generalization}), the reference model remains fixed even when GPT-2~XL is held out; the partial architectural overlap this introduces is discussed in Limitations.
41	
42	\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}
43	\label{sec:method:features}
44	
45	We extract a 19-dimensional feature vector $\mathbf{f}(x)$ from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$.
46	The features fall into four categories (Table~\ref{tab:features}).
47	
48	\begin{table}[t]
49	\centering
50	\small
51	\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).}
52	\label{tab:features}
53	\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}
54	\toprule
55	Category & Features & $n$ \\
56	\midrule
57	Perplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\
58	statistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\
59	\addlinespace
60	Surprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\
61	statistics & & \\
62	\addlinespace
63	Lexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\
64	diversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\
65	\addlinespace
66	Repetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\
67	\bottomrule
68	\end{tabular}
69	\end{table}
70	
71	\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments (mean, variance, skewness, kurtosis) and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles prove especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\S\ref{sec:atlas}).
72	\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\log p(\text{token})$; surprisal variance captures second-order structure not redundant with perplexity moments.
73	\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.
74	\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \in \{2, 3, 4\}$.
75	
76	\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}
77	\label{sec:method:edd}
78	
79	Aggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.
80	We introduce the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) to formalize the frontier beyond which adjacent depths become statistically indistinguishable.
81	
82	\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}
83	Let $\mathcal{D} = \{0, 1, \dots, K\}$ denote the depth set and $T \in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.
84	For each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\lfloor\sqrt{19}\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\S\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.
85	Each pair uses an independent classifier, avoiding the decision boundary dilution that arises in multi-class settings (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
86	
87	\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]
88	\label{def:edd}
89	The \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:
90	\begin{equation}
91	\mathrm{EDD}_T = \max \bigl\{ d^*{+}1 \,:\, \forall\, d' \leq d^*,\; \mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \geq T \bigr\}
92	\label{eq:edd}
93	\end{equation}
94	where $d^* \in \{0, \dots, K{-}1\}$. If\, $\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.
95	\end{definition}
96	
97	$\mathrm{EDD}_T$ is the deepest depth $d$ such that every adjacent pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ remains discriminable at level $T$.
98	The universal quantifier ensures EDD reports the longest \emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix rather than an isolated high-AUC pair at greater depth.\footnote{Our AUC profiles are empirically monotonically decreasing (\S\ref{sec:atlas}), so in practice $\mathrm{EDD}_T = \max\{d'{+}1 : \mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \geq T\}$. For non-monotonic profiles, the contiguous formulation is more conservative (see Limitations).}
99	We set $T{=}0.75$ as default; sensitivity at $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$ is reported in \S\ref{sec:atlas}.
100	
101	\paragraph{Classifiers.}
102	Our primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold stratified cross-validation).
103	We compare against CORAL ordinal regression \cite{coral2020}, DivEye \cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \S\ref{sec:generalization}.
```

transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset as seed text ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each generation cycle proceeds in three steps: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$, freezing base model weights and training low-rank update matrices ($<$1\\% additional parameters; $r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$, architecture-specific targets); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages from the LoRA-finetuned model (max 512 tokens, BOS-seeded, no prompt conditioning) under the specified decoding strategy; (3)~the generated corpus becomes $\\mathcal{D}_k$ for the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model explicitly trained on the prior depth's data---not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nThere is no per-passage lineage across depths, ensuring cross-validation splits do not introduce information leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) are computed using a fixed reference model---GPT-2 base (124M)---that is independent of the three generation models.\nThe reference model computes per-token perplexity for each passage, providing a consistent measurement frame across model families.\nIn cross-model leave-one-out evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}), the reference model remains fixed even when GPT-2~XL is held out; the partial architectural overlap this introduces is discussed in Limitations.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a 19-dimensional feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments (mean, variance, skewness, kurtosis) and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles prove especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$; surprisal variance captures second-order structure not redundant with perplexity moments.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) to formalize the frontier beyond which adjacent depths become statistically indistinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\nEach pair uses an independent classifier, avoiding the decision boundary dilution that arises in multi-class settings (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$. If\\, $\\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.\n\\end{definition}\n\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ is the deepest depth $d$ such that every adjacent pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ remains discriminable at level $T$.\nThe universal quantifier ensures EDD reports the longest \\emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix rather than an isolated high-AUC pair at greater depth.\\footnote{Our AUC profiles are empirically monotonically decreasing (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), so in practice $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max\\{d'{+}1 : \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T\\}$. For non-monotonic profiles, the contiguous formulation is more conservative (see Limitations).}\nWe set $T{=}0.75$ as default; sensitivity at $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$ is reported in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas}.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold stratified cross-validation).\nWe compare against CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",

1	\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}
2	\label{sec:atlas}
3	
4	We present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.
5	The atlas spans $3 \times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells evaluated at depths $d \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
6	
7	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
8	\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}
9	\label{sec:atlas:id}
10	
11	Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.
12	Accuracy ranges from 69.6\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.4\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature), all exceeding the 25\% chance baseline by a wide margin.
13	Two axis effects emerge.
14	GPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (76.6--85.4\%), followed by OLMo (74.2--82.0\%) and Pythia (69.6--78.2\%).
15	Top-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.
16	The mean within-model strategy gap is 8.4~pp, comparable to the 7.1~pp mean within-strategy model gap---decoding strategy and model family contribute roughly equally to performance variance.
17	
18	\begin{table}[t]
19	\centering
20	\small
21	\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\%) across the $3 \times 3$ atlas, with d1--d3 three-class accuracy in parentheses (chance = 25\% / 33\%). Depth-0 recall exceeds 98\% in all cells.}
22	\label{tab:main_matrix}
23	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
24	\toprule
25	& Nucleus & Temp.\ $\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\
26	\midrule
27	Pythia-1.4B  & 78.2\,(71.2) & 76.3\,(70.6) & 69.6\,(58.8) \\
28	OLMo-1B     & 78.8\,(75.0) & 82.0\,(76.3) & 74.2\,(66.1) \\
29	GPT-2~XL    & 83.3\,(79.0) & 85.4\,(82.6) & 76.6\,(69.5) \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\end{tabular}
32	\end{table}
33	
34	\paragraph{Per-class recall distribution.}
35	Depth-0 recall exceeds 98\% in all 9 cells, confirming that human-written text is trivially separable from any generation depth.
36	Among generated depths, d2 consistently yields the lowest recall (46--73\% across cells), followed by d3 (58--82\%) and d1 (72--91\%).
37	For example, Pythia-1.4B nucleus achieves per-class recall of d0$\,{=}\,$99\%, d1$\,{=}\,$82\%, d2$\,{=}\,$59\%, d3$\,{=}\,$72\%.
38	The strongest d2 recall is obtained by GPT-2~XL nucleus (73\%).
39	The d2 trough reflects its position at the center of the depth gradient: d2 features overlap with both the d1 distribution above and d3 below, making it the most confusable class---consistent with the d2-vs-d3 pair being the EDD bottleneck (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
40	Full per-class recall, precision, and F1 for all cells appear in Appendix~\ref{app:per_class}.
41	
42	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
43	\subsection{Feature Universality}
44	\label{sec:atlas:features}
45	
46	Despite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.
47	The 90th-percentile perplexity (\texttt{p90\_ppl}) ranks top-2 by Gini importance in all 9 cells; surprisal variance (\texttt{var\_surprisal}) in 7/9 (8/9 under SHAP; Appendix~\ref{app:permutation}).
48	Table~\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.
49	
50	Cross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\bar{\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.
51	Among strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).
52	
53	Percentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.
54	Token-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \texttt{p90\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals generation depth.
55	
56	Feature ablation confirms this concentration: the top-5 features achieve 97.6\% of full accuracy (76.5\% vs.\ 78.4\%), with perplexity statistics alone reaching 76.6\% (vs.\ surprisal 73.2\%, other categories $<$46\%).
57	Forward selection independently confirms the dominant pair: \texttt{var\_surprisal} is selected first in 6/9 cells, \texttt{p90\_ppl} in 3/9.
58	
59	The feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.
60	At shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\ d2), \texttt{var\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\ d4, d4 vs.\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{kurtosis\_ppl}) take over.
61	This transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).
62

89	\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}

91	
92	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
93	\subsection{Monotonicity and Inter-Depth Distances}
94	\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}
95	
96	Depth features exhibit strong monotonic trends: perplexity tail statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_surprisal}) decrease with depth (Spearman $\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians) while repetition features increase, reflecting the complementary narrowing and self-repetition of distribution collapse.
97	Inter-depth distances are non-uniform: d0-vs-d1 AUC exceeds 0.99 in all 9 cells, whereas d2-vs-d3 ranges from 0.69 to 0.89---separating adjacent deep generations requires the tail statistics identified in \S\ref{sec:atlas:features}.
98	
99	\begin{figure}[t]

102	\caption{Feature trajectories across depths $d{=}0$ to $d{=}3$, stratified by model (columns) and strategy (line style). Top row: \texttt{p90\_ppl} (monotonically decreasing). Middle: \texttt{var\_surprisal} (decreasing). Bottom: \texttt{rep\_3gram} (increasing). Shaded bands: interquartile range. The $d{=}0 \to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}
103	\label{fig:feature_trajectory}
104	\end{figure}
105	
106	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
107	\subsection{EDD Analysis}
108	\label{sec:atlas:edd}
109	
110	We extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.
111	Each model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
112	We train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\ref{eq:edd}.
113	
114	\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}
115	Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.
116	For Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.
117	The steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.
118	
119	\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}
120	The d3-vs-d4 AUC consistently clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold across all three models (Pythia $0.784$, OLMo $0.765$, GPT-2~XL $0.750$), while d4-vs-d5 uniformly falls below $0.70$ ($0.681$--$0.684$).
121	The directional pattern is consistent, but statistical strength is stratified: bootstrap testing ($10{,}000$ resamples, $H_0{:}\, \mathrm{AUC} \leq 0.75$) yields $p = 0.001$ for Pythia (significant), $p = 0.091$ for OLMo (marginal), and $p = 0.505$ for GPT-2~XL (non-significant).
122	All three models cross the $T{=}0.75$ threshold within the d3--d4 region, establishing a consistent depth-4 decay boundary with graded confidence rather than uniform statistical significance.
123	The d4-vs-d5 saturation is unambiguous across models, supporting the interpretation of d3--d4 as a \emph{transition zone} from discriminable to indistinguishable regime.
124	
125	\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}
126	Table~\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.
127	At $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\mathrm{EDD} \geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.
128	The cells losing $\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the consistent bottleneck across all 9 cells.
129

All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\% CIs.}
148	\label{fig:auc_decay}
149	\end{figure}
150	
151	\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}
152	\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}
153	EDD uses binary-pair classifiers to avoid \emph{decision boundary dilution}: in Pythia nucleus, d2\,vs.\,d3 AUC degrades from 0.804 (4-class) to 0.689 (6-class)---a 14.3\% relative decline despite identical data---because shared decision boundaries dilute per-pair capacity.
154	Binary classifiers eliminate this confound, isolating true adjacent-pair discriminability.
155	
156	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
157	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}
158	\label{sec:atlas:strategy}
159	
160	The three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories.
161	Nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) produces the most consistent accuracy (78.2--83.3\%) and serves as the reference trajectory.
162	Temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$) yields the highest accuracy in 2/3 models (GPT-2~XL 85.4\%, OLMo 82.0\%) because uniform logit scaling accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint---but this acceleration creates a boundary reversal risk: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features, causing cross-strategy classifiers to systematically overpredict depth (\S\ref{sec:generalization}).
163	Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$) produces the lowest accuracy (69.6--76.6\%) because hard vocabulary truncation removes depth-discriminative low-probability tokens, though feature direction is preserved (cross-model $\rho = 0.811$).
164	The strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp), indicating deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy matters at least as much as the generator model.
165

Depth-0 recall exceeds 98\\% in all cells.}\n\\label{tab:main_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& Nucleus & Temp.\\ $\\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B  & 78.2\\,(71.2) & 76.3\\,(70.6) & 69.6\\,(58.8) \\\\\nOLMo-1B     & 78.8\\,(75.0) & 82.0\\,(76.3) & 74.2\\,(66.1) \\\\\nGPT-2~XL    & 83.3\\,(79.0) & 85.4\\,(82.6) & 76.6\\,(69.5) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-class recall distribution.}\nDepth-0 recall exceeds 98\\% in all 9 cells, confirming that human-written text is trivially separable from any generation depth.\nAmong generated depths, d2 consistently yields the lowest recall (46--73\\% across cells), followed by d3 (58--82\\%) and d1 (72--91\\%).\nFor example, Pythia-1.4B nucleus achieves per-class recall of d0$\\,{=}\\,$99\\%, d1$\\,{=}\\,$82\\%, d2$\\,{=}\\,$59\\%, d3$\\,{=}\\,$72\\%.\nThe strongest d2 recall is obtained by GPT-2~XL nucleus (73\\%).\nThe d2 trough reflects its position at the center of the depth gradient: d2 features overlap with both the d1 distribution above and d3 below, making it the most confusable class---consistent with the d2-vs-d3 pair being the EDD bottleneck (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\nFull per-class recall, precision, and F1 for all cells appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:per_class}.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Feature Universality}\n\\label{sec:atlas:features}\n\nDespite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.\nThe 90th-percentile perplexity (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) ranks top-2 by Gini importance in all 9 cells; surprisal variance (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) in 7/9 (8/9 under SHAP; Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.\n\nCross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\\bar{\\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.\nAmong strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\n\nPercentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.\nToken-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals generation depth.\n\nFeature ablation confirms this concentration: the top-5 features achieve 97.6\\% of full accuracy (76.5\\% vs.\\ 78.4\\%), with perplexity statistics alone reaching 76.6\\% (vs.\\ surprisal 73.2\\%, other categories $<$46\\%).\nForward selection independently confirms the dominant pair: \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} is selected first in 6/9 cells, \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} in 3/9.\n\nThe feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.\nAt shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\\ d2), \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\\ d4, d4 vs.\\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) take over.\nThis transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Top-5 features by Gini importance. \\textbf{Bold}: top-2. \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9; \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} 7/9 (8/9 under SHAP).}\n\\label{tab:feature_importance}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & Top-5 features (Gini importance, ranked) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc.       & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.15)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), var\\_ppl\\,(.08), p75\\,(.07) \\\\\nPythia, $\\tau$     & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.14)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), p75\\,(.07), m\\_surp\\,(.07) \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$    & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.16)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.12)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), var\\_ppl\\,(.07), m\\_surp\\,(.06) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, nuc.    & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.21)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.21)}, p75\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.07), m\\_ppl\\,(.06) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, $\\tau$  & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.25)}, \\textbf{p75\\,(.15)}, m\\_surp\\,(.13), var\\_surp\\,(.12), p50\\,(.05) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, top-$k$ & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.24)}, \\textbf{p75\\,(.12)}, var\\_surp\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.09), ttr\\,(.04) \\\\\nOLMo, nuc.         & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.18)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.15)}, p75\\,(.11), m\\_surp\\,(.07), ttr\\,(.06) \\\\\nOLMo, $\\tau$       & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.21)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.16)}, p75\\,(.12), m\\_surp\\,(.09), ttr\\,(.05) \\\\\nOLMo, top-$k$      & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.20)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, p75\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.07), m\\_ppl\\,(.05) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_atlas_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap. Rows: 19 features grouped by category (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}); columns: 9 model--strategy cells. Color intensity encodes RF Gini importance. The perplexity-tail and surprisal-variance blocks maintain high importance across all cells.}\n\\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Monotonicity and Inter-Depth Distances}\n\\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}\n\nDepth features exhibit strong monotonic trends: perplexity tail statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_surprisal}) decrease with depth (Spearman $\\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians) while repetition features increase, reflecting the complementary narrowing and self-repetition of distribution collapse.\nInter-depth distances are non-uniform: d0-vs-d1 AUC exceeds 0.99 in all 9 cells, whereas d2-vs-d3 ranges from 0.69 to 0.89---separating adjacent deep generations requires the tail statistics identified in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_feature_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across depths $d{=}0$ to $d{=}3$, stratified by model (columns) and strategy (line style). Top row: \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (monotonically decreasing). Middle: \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} (decreasing). Bottom: \\texttt{rep\\_3gram} (increasing). Shaded bands: interquartile range. The $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}\n\\label{fig:feature_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{EDD Analysis}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd}\n\nWe extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.\nEach model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).\nWe train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}.\n\n\\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.\nFor Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.\nThe steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}\nThe d3-vs-d4 AUC consistently clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold across all three models (Pythia $0.784$, OLMo $0.765$, GPT-2~XL $0.750$), while d4-vs-d5 uniformly falls below $0.70$ ($0.681$--$0.684$).\nThe directional pattern is consistent, but statistical strength is stratified: bootstrap testing ($10{,}000$ resamples, $H_0{:}\\, \\mathrm{AUC} \\leq 0.75$) yields $p = 0.001$ for Pythia (significant), $p = 0.091$ for OLMo (marginal), and $p = 0.505$ for GPT-2~XL (non-significant).\nAll three models cross the $T{=}0.75$ threshold within the d3--d4 region, establishing a consistent depth-4 decay boundary with graded confidence rather than uniform statistical significance.\nThe d4-vs-d5 saturation is unambiguous across models, supporting the interpretation of d3--d4 as a \\emph{transition zone} from discriminable to indistinguishable regime.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.\nAt $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.\nThe cells losing $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the consistent bottleneck across all 9 cells.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). The d2-vs-d3 boundary is the bottleneck; top-$k$ cells drop first.}\n\\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $T{=}0.65$ & $T{=}0.70$ & $T{=}0.75$ & $T{=}0.80$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCells with $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ & 9/9 & 8/9 & 7/9 & 5/9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}\nEDD uses binary-pair classifiers to avoid \\emph{decision boundary dilution}: in Pythia nucleus, d2\\,vs.\\,d3 AUC degrades from 0.804 (4-class) to 0.689 (6-class)---a 14.3\\% relative decline despite identical data---because shared decision boundaries dilute per-pair capacity.\nBinary classifiers eliminate this confound, isolating true adjacent-pair discriminability.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}\n\nThe three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories.\nNucleus ($p{=}0.95$) produces the most consistent accuracy (78.2--83.3\\%) and serves as the reference trajectory.\nTemperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$) yields the highest accuracy in 2/3 models (GPT-2~XL 85.4\\%, OLMo 82.0\\%) because uniform logit scaling accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint---but this acceleration creates a boundary reversal risk: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features, causing cross-strategy classifiers to systematically overpredict depth (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nTop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) produces the lowest accuracy (69.6--76.6\\%) because hard vocabulary truncation removes depth-discriminative low-probability tokens, though feature direction is preserved (cross-model $\\rho = 0.811$).\nThe strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp), indicating deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy matters at least as much as the generator model.\n",
      "numLines": 165,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 165
    }
  }
}

1	\section{Generalization and Robustness}
2	\label{sec:generalization}
3	
4	We test whether the atlas results (69.6--85.4\%) reflect genuine statistical regularities or training artifacts, evaluating four axes: classifier choice (\S\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and end-to-end learning (\S\ref{sec:gen:e2e}).
5	
6	\subsection{Baseline Comparison}
7	\label{sec:gen:baselines}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:baselines} evaluates three classifier families---bagging (RF), gradient boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM)---on the same 19 features across three atlas cells spanning the full accuracy range.
10	Per-cell spread is $\leq$0.4pp, establishing a feature-determined ceiling independent of the learning algorithm.
11	On Pythia nucleus, CORAL ordinal regression \cite{coral2020} (78.1\%) and DivEye \cite{diveye2026} (78.2\%) match the within-cell RF of Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix} within 0.1\,pp (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected); all feature-based methods significantly outperform CE-MLP (74.9\%, $p<0.001$) and logistic regression (75.0\%).
12	Single-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\%); a zero-shot LLM (DeepSeek V3.2) reaches only 36.2\% (95\% CI $[32.0, 40.4]$\%, 500 samples).

94	\label{sec:gen:e2e}
95	
96	DeBERTa-v3-base \cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end achieve 58.3\% ($\pm$4.7\%) and 59.5\% ($\pm$3.2\%) respectively---lagging the RF baseline (78.2\%) by 19.9pp and 18.7pp.
97	The 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias; Table~\ref{tab:e2e_detail} shows both backbones share the same failure mode---high d0 F1 ($>$0.94) but collapsed d1/d2 performance (F1 $\leq$ 0.42)---while RF achieves 0.596 d2 recall, a $>$2$\times$ advantage at the hardest depth class.

116	
117	\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}
118	SHAP TreeExplainer shows perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\% of RF's contribution (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$ across three importance measures, $p{<}0.005$; Appendix~\ref{app:permutation}).
119	Both PLM backbones correlate \emph{negatively} with RF importance (DeBERTa $\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap), with linear probing yielding negative $R^2$ for \texttt{var\_surprisal} and \texttt{p90\_ppl} (Appendix~\ref{app:feature_overlap})---a systematic representational gap, not backbone-specific artifacts.
120	
121	DCOL \cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\% (Appendix~\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from explicit tail statistics absent from PLM representations.
122	This comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.
123	
124	\paragraph{Ecological validity.}
125	On WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages), all three model-specific RF classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\% domain shift in mean perplexity---partly an easy case, as WikiText's higher perplexity places it farther from d1--d3 in feature space.
126	On noisier web-crawl data (5{,}000 C4 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\% (OLMo) to 22.4\% (GPT-2~XL), concentrated in template/SEO content with high n-gram repetition (Table~\ref{tab:calibration}); deployment on heterogeneous web data requires model-specific calibration.

      "content": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether the atlas results (69.6--85.4\\%) reflect genuine statistical regularities or training artifacts, evaluating four axes: classifier choice (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and end-to-end learning (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:e2e}).\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Comparison}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} evaluates three classifier families---bagging (RF), gradient boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM)---on the same 19 features across three atlas cells spanning the full accuracy range.\nPer-cell spread is $\\leq$0.4pp, establishing a feature-determined ceiling independent of the learning algorithm.\nOn Pythia nucleus, CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020} (78.1\\%) and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} (78.2\\%) match the within-cell RF of Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} within 0.1\\,pp (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected); all feature-based methods significantly outperform CE-MLP (74.9\\%, $p<0.001$) and logistic regression (75.0\\%).\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%); a zero-shot LLM (DeepSeek V3.2) reaches only 36.2\\% (95\\% CI $[32.0, 40.4]$\\%, 500 samples).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Classifier convergence: 4-class accuracy (\\%) across three atlas cells. RF (bagging), XGBoost and LightGBM (gradient boosting) operate on the same 19 features; $\\Delta_{\\max}$: largest per-cell spread. Chance = 25\\%. RF values differ from Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} by $\\leq$0.7pp due to matched-fold evaluation.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & RF & XGB & LGBM & $\\Delta_{\\max}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc. & 78.8 & 79.0 & 78.9 & 0.2 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, $\\tau$ & 85.8 & 86.2 & 86.2 & 0.4 \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$ & 68.9 & 68.9 & 68.7 & 0.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nConvergence across five structurally different classifiers---spanning bagging, gradient boosting, ordinal regression, and ensemble detection---confirms the signal resides in the features, not the learning algorithm.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 near-perfectly identified; errors concentrate at d2/d3 in-distribution and spread across d1--d3 under transfer.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d1--d3 bal.\\ acc: Pythia 49.9\\%, OLMo 59.2\\%, GPT-2~XL 57.2\\% (mean 55.4\\%).}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy is 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance); d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline while d2 drops to 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout).\nExcluding d0, d1--d3 balanced accuracy averages 55.4\\% (chance = 33\\%).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nWe train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d0 recall $\\geq$89\\% inflates headline; d1--d3 mean: 45.9\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7pp below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%); excluding d0, d1--d3 accuracy drops to 45.9\\% (chance = 33\\%).\nd2 recall varies sharply: nucleus holdouts retain 30--54\\%, top-$k$ holdouts fall to 8--19\\%, and temperature holdouts span 3.7--39.5\\% (median 8.9\\%).\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature inverts the d1/d2 boundary (d1v2 AUC $<$ 0.5); per-strategy z-score normalization partially recovers it but leaves a residual gap (Limitations).\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{sec:gen:e2e}\n\nDeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end achieve 58.3\\% ($\\pm$4.7\\%) and 59.5\\% ($\\pm$3.2\\%) respectively---lagging the RF baseline (78.2\\%) by 19.9pp and 18.7pp.\nThe 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e_detail} shows both backbones share the same failure mode---high d0 F1 ($>$0.94) but collapsed d1/d2 performance (F1 $\\leq$ 0.42)---while RF achieves 0.596 d2 recall, a $>$2$\\times$ advantage at the hardest depth class.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class performance for end-to-end PLM classifiers vs.\\ RF baseline (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). PLM columns report F1; RF d2 column reports recall (the class with the largest PLM--RF gap). Both PLM backbones share the same failure pattern: collapsed d1/d2 scores despite adequate d0/d3.}\n\\label{tab:e2e_detail}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Acc (\\%) & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeBERTa-v3 & 58.3\\,$\\pm$\\,4.7 & .947 & .378 & .247 & .656 \\\\\nRoBERTa-base & 59.5\\,$\\pm$\\,3.2 & .958 & .416 & .261 & .647 \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 feat.) & 78.2 & --- & --- & .596$^\\dagger$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Recall; shown for the depth class with the largest PLM--RF gap.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}\nSHAP TreeExplainer shows perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\\% of RF's contribution (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$ across three importance measures, $p{<}0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}).\nBoth PLM backbones correlate \\emph{negatively} with RF importance (DeBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap), with linear probing yielding negative $R^2$ for \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap})---a systematic representational gap, not backbone-specific artifacts.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from explicit tail statistics absent from PLM representations.\nThis comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Ecological validity.}\nOn WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages), all three model-specific RF classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\\% domain shift in mean perplexity---partly an easy case, as WikiText's higher perplexity places it farther from d1--d3 in feature space.\nOn noisier web-crawl data (5{,}000 C4 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL), concentrated in template/SEO content with high n-gram repetition (Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}); deployment on heterogeneous web data requires model-specific calibration.",

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	We formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that 19 statistical features achieve 70--85\% four-class accuracy ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$), with five features sufficing for 97.6\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\leq$0.4pp.
5	The dominant pair (\texttt{p90\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 7/9) generalizes across model families without retraining, making depth estimation a measurable early warning for model collapse~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}---though cross-strategy transfer remains the bottleneck (59.1\% vs.\ 66.4\%), web-crawl deployment requires model-specific calibration (C4 FPR: 4.7--22.4\%; Table~\ref{tab:calibration}), and all experiments use 1--1.5B models.
6	Future work should validate on mixed-domain corpora at larger scales and evaluate whether depth-graduated curation improves downstream training outcomes.
7	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PA8CjfckyuybB2DJBmj1Sb`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}
5	Cross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.
6	Per-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\to}0.70$) but leaves a residual gap relative to within-strategy performance (${\sim}0.83$), indicating that a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.
7	
8	\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}
9	Depth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\% across all settings), inflating headline accuracy.
10	Cross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).
11	
12	\paragraph{Controlled vs.\ ecological setting.}
13	All experiments use Wikipedia seed text with controlled recursive generation (LoRA fine-tuning, 1--1.5B parameter models).
14	Depth signal is the dominant classification factor (5-fold CV 59--82\%, up to 2.5$\times$ chance): LOGO accuracy (72.1\%) matches standard CV (72.0\%), and seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\% vs.\ 10\%; Appendix~\ref{app:domain_bias}).
15	Transfer to instruction-tuned models and larger scales (7B+) remains open.
16	Real-world documents may also interleave segments at different recursive generation depths; extending to segment-level estimation introduces a granularity--statistical-power tradeoff.
17	
18	\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}
19	On editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\% (OLMo) to 22.4\% (GPT-2~XL).
20	Misclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\texttt{rep\_3gram} $+$37\%, \texttt{rep\_4gram} $+$75\% vs.\ correctly classified d0); the model gap likely reflects training data composition (OLMo's Dolma includes diverse web data vs.\ Pythia/GPT-2~XL's Wikipedia/WebText).
21	Deployment on heterogeneous web corpora requires model- and domain-specific calibration.
22	
23	\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}
24	Adversarial paraphrasing \cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.
25	
26	\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}
27	Our EDD definition (Eq.~\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically---as observed in all our configurations.
28	However, \citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).
29	Our cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.
30	In such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.
31	The choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.
32	
33	\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}
34	Surprisal-based features are computed relative to GPT-2 base (124M), introducing partial overlap when GPT-2~XL is held out, though GPT-2~XL holdout accuracy (67.6\%) does not exceed the unrelated OLMo (69.2\%).
35	The choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.
36	
37	\paragraph{Additional caveats.}
38	Detection features degrade under domain shift \cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}; our depth features are likely susceptible.
39	\citet{falahati2026_curated} show that curated synthetic data need not trigger collapse, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.
40	
41	\section*{Ethics Statement}
42	
43	This work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.
44	No human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.
45	Our method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.
46	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
```

1	\section{Additional Results}
2	\label{app:additional}
3	
4	\subsection{Top-$k$ Feature Trajectories}
5	\label{app:topk_trajectories}
6	
7	Figure~\ref{fig:feature_trajectory} in the main text shows feature trajectories for nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) and temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$) sampling.
8	Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$) trajectories follow the same monotonically decreasing trend for all features but with compressed inter-depth spacing, consistent with the signal attenuation effect discussed in \S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.
9	Concretely, the d0$\to$d1 drop in \textsc{p90\_ppl} under top-$k$ is 78\% (vs.\ 87\% for nucleus), and the d2$\to$d3 reduction is 31\% (vs.\ 39\% for nucleus).
10	Despite the magnitude reduction, feature rank ordering across depths is preserved, which explains why ordinal classifiers maintain directionality even under top-$k$ conditions.
11	
12	\subsection{Permutation Importance}
13	\label{app:permutation}
14	
15	To verify that the Gini-based importance rankings in \S\ref{sec:atlas:features} are not an artifact of the impurity measure, we compute permutation importance (5-fold CV, 10 repeats) for all 9 atlas cells.
16	The top-2 features are stable across measures: \textsc{p90\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in 9/9 cells under both Gini and permutation importance, and \textsc{var\_surprisal} in 8/9 (vs.\ 7/9 for Gini, with the additional cell being OLMo top-$k$; the single SHAP exception is GPT-2~XL top-$k$, where \textsc{p75\_ppl} displaces \textsc{var\_surprisal} to rank 3).
17	Kendall's $W{=}0.90$ across all three methods (Gini, permutation, SHAP) indicates strong concordance ($p{<}0.005$).
18	Pairwise Spearman correlations are: SHAP--Gini $\rho{=}0.93$, SHAP--permutation $\rho{=}0.84$, Gini--permutation $\rho{=}0.76$.
19	Per-cell top-5 overlap between SHAP and permutation importance is 3--4 out of 5 features; the aggregated (mean-across-cells) top-5 sets are identical, confirming that the top features are robust to the choice of importance measure at the aggregate level while per-cell rankings exhibit model-dependent variation.
20	
21	\subsection{Per-Class Recall}
22	\label{app:per_class}
23	
24	Table~\ref{tab:per_class_recall} reports per-class recall across all 9 atlas cells (4-class in-distribution, 5-fold CV).
25	Depth~0 recall exceeds 98\% uniformly.
26	Among generated depths, d2 is the hardest class in every cell; the deficit is most pronounced under top-$k$ sampling (46--53\%), where vocabulary truncation removes the tail tokens that carry depth-discriminative signal.
27

29	\centering
30	\small
31	\caption{Per-class recall (\%) for all 9 atlas cells. d2 is the lowest-recall class in every cell. Boldface marks the highest d2 recall.}

35	Cell & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\

38	Pythia, $\tau$     & 99.7 & 81.3 & 53.9 & 72.1 \\
39	Pythia, top-$k$    & 99.5 & 72.3 & 45.6 & 57.5 \\

41	OLMo, $\tau$       & 99.7 & 87.7 & 66.1 & 77.2 \\
42	OLMo, top-$k$      & 99.5 & 78.5 & 51.2 & 65.6 \\

47	\end{tabular}

49	
50	\subsection{DCOL Iteration Details}
51	\label{app:dcol_iterations}
52	
53	Table~\ref{tab:dcol_iterations} summarizes all six DCOL iterations.
54	DeBERTa-v3-large exhibited systematic numerical instability across five configurations; RoBERTa-large resolved stability but could not close the accuracy gap with feature-based methods.
55
