59	\caption{DCOL iteration summary. All experiments use Pythia-1.4B nucleus data. RF baseline: 78.2\%.}
60	\label{tab:dcol_iterations}
61	\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}
62	\toprule
63	Ver. & Backbone & Config & Outcome \\
64	\midrule
65	v1 & DeBERTa & FT, m\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\ collapse (31.5\%) \\
66	v2 & DeBERTa & FT, m\_lr $10^{-4}$ & OOM \\
67	v3 & DeBERTa & FT, m\_lr $10^{-4}$ & Forgetting + OOM \\
68	v4 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\ collapse \\
69	v5 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\_lr $10^{-4}$ & NaN gradients \\
70	v6 & RoBERTa & FT, grad clip 1.0 & Stable; 64.5\% ($-$13.7pp) \\
71	\bottomrule
72	\end{tabular}
73	\end{table}
74	
75	\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}
76	\label{app:feature_overlap}
77	
78	We probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features.
79	DeBERTa attention importance and RF permutation importance exhibit a significant negative correlation (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.484$, $p{=}0.036$; top-5 Jaccard overlap$\,{=}\,0$).
80	PLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\textsc{var\_surprisal} and \textsc{p90\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing.
81	This confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\S\ref{sec:gen:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.
82	
83	\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}
84	\label{app:domain_bias}
85	
86	To assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.
87	
88	\paragraph{Methodological note.}
89	An initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\%).
90	We discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.
91	The results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.
92	
93	\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}
94	Five-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\% to 82\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\times$ above the 33.3\% chance level), confirming that depth signal is the dominant classification factor.
95	
96	\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}
97	We assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.
98	LOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\% (range 59.5--81.6\%), matching standard CV (72.0\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).
99	Per-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp, indicating that cross-group generalization is as reliable as within-group classification.
100	
101	\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}
102	We train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\%).
103	Depth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\%---indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).
104	The depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\times$, confirming that the feature representation encodes depth but not seed-article identity.
105	
106	Taken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.
107	
108	\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}
109	\label{app:calibration}
110	
111	Table~\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages).
112	
113	\begin{table}[h]
114	\centering
115	\small
116	\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+.}
117	\label{tab:calibration}
118	\begin{tabular}{@{}llrcc@{}}
119	\toprule
120	Model & Source & $n$ & d0 (\%) & FPR (\%) \\
121	\midrule
122	Pythia-1.4B & WikiText & 546 & 100.0 & 0.0 \\
123	 & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 \\
124	\addlinespace
125	GPT-2~XL & WikiText & 546 & 99.3 & 0.7 \\
126	 & C4 & 5{,}000 & 77.6 & 22.4 \\
127	\addlinespace
128	OLMo-1B & WikiText & 546 & 98.5 & 1.5 \\
129	 & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 \\
130	\bottomrule
131	\end{tabular}
132	\end{table}
133	
134	On WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy, confirming that editorial text is reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.
135	On C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\%) vs.\ Pythia (20.2\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\%).
136	C4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\texttt{rep\_3gram} $+$37\%, \texttt{rep\_4gram} $+$75\% vs.\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.
137	The model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.
138	Among C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\%), d2 (3.7\%), d3 (9.6\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\%), d2 (0.5\%), d3 (1.3\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.
139	
140	\subsection{Extended Discussion}
141	\label{app:extended_discussion}
142	
143	\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}
144	Current data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.
145	Recursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.
146	The 70--85\% in-distribution accuracy and $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest such depth-graduated filtering is feasible across the model--strategy configurations we study, though its downstream effect on training quality remains to be validated empirically.
147	Importance weighting \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.
148	
149	\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}
150	Depth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.
151	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.
152	Source attribution \cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.
153	Depth estimation characterizes \emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.
154	A layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.
155

docs/paper/discussion.tex:4:We formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that 19 statistical features achieve 70--85\% four-class accuracy ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$), with five features sufficing for 97.6\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\leq$0.4pp.
docs/paper/method.tex:45:We extract a 19-dimensional feature vector $\mathbf{f}(x)$ from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$.
docs/paper/method.tex:51:\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).}
docs/paper/abstract.tex:4:From each passage we extract 19 statistical features---perplexity percentiles, surprisal moments, and lexical diversity measures---computed against a fixed reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
docs/paper/introduction.tex:18:Given a text passage, we compute a 19-dimensional feature vector from reference-model perplexity statistics---capturing distributional location, spread, tail behavior, and lexical diversity---and classify it with a Random Forest into one of $K{+}1$ depth classes ($d{=}0$ for human text through $d{=}K$ for $K$-th generation text).

45:GPT-2~XL, top-$k$  & 98.5 & 79.5 & 52.5 & 71.1 \\

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nAs large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\\to$data$\\to$model loop arises---yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.\nWe formalize \\emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.\nFrom each passage we extract 19 statistical features---perplexity percentiles, surprisal moments, and lexical diversity measures---computed against a fixed reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).\nA Random Forest classifier achieves 70--85\\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\\%; 58.8--82.6\\% when excluding the near-perfectly separable d0 class; a zero-shot LLM baseline reaches only 36.2\\%), with 90th-percentile perplexity and surprisal variance emerging as consistent depth markers across the studied $3 \\times 3$ grid (top-2 importance in 9/9 and 7/9 cells, respectively).\nOur \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas} reveals that decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories rather than merely attenuating them, and we define the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for extended recursion chains.\nCross-model transfer reaches 66.4\\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\\%; temperature sampling induces a d1/d2 AUC inversion that we decompose into a correctable scale component and a residual structural component.\nApplied to WikiText-103 (546 passages), all three model-specific classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\\% domain shift in mean perplexity; on C4 web-crawl text, false positive rates range from 4.7\\% to 22.4\\%, concentrated in template content.\n\\end{abstract}",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nThe text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.\nAs synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.\nThis model$\\to$data$\\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical---it is already occurring in the wild---yet we lack the ability to determine how many recursive iterations a given text has undergone, a distinction that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.\n\nExisting tools address adjacent but distinct problems.\nBinary detectors such as Binoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, Fast-DetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} classify text as human-written or machine-generated, collapsing all depths $d \\geq 1$ into a single category.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding and cannot be applied retroactively.\nThe model collapse literature \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.\nNo existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone.\n\nOur key insight is that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are consistent across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.\nSpecifically, perplexity tail statistics---the 90th-percentile perplexity (\\textsc{p90\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance (\\textsc{var\\_surprisal})---emerge as dominant depth indicators, ranking in the top two features by importance in 9/9 and 8/9 model--strategy configurations, respectively.\n\nWe formalize recursive generation depth estimation as an ordinal classification task and introduce a lightweight pipeline to solve it.\nGiven a text passage, we compute a 19-dimensional feature vector from reference-model perplexity statistics---capturing distributional location, spread, tail behavior, and lexical diversity---and classify it with a Random Forest into one of $K{+}1$ depth classes ($d{=}0$ for human text through $d{=}K$ for $K$-th generation text).\nTo systematically characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and decoding strategies (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, temperature $\\tau{=}0.9$, top-$k$ $k{=}50$), each spanning 4 generation depths, totaling 180K samples.\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories---temperature sampling accelerates convergence toward a low-entropy attractor, while top-$k$ attenuates the depth signal---and we introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and introduce the EDD metric, which summarizes the maximum depth at which adjacent generations remain separable above a given AUC threshold.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} We construct a systematic $3 \\times 3$ map across model families and decoding strategies, revealing that \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in all 9 cells and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9, with a mean Spearman rank correlation of $\\rho{=}0.868$ across importance vectors. The extended Pythia nucleus pipeline (d0--d5) yields $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$, establishing that depth discrimination persists through four recursive steps before falling below threshold.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Generalization analysis.} Cross-model leave-one-out transfer achieves 66.4\\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\\% (chance = 25\\%). We decompose a temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion into a scale component---partially correctable by per-strategy normalization---and a residual structural component that simple normalization cannot resolve.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{End-to-end learning.} Both DeBERTa-v3-base (58.3\\%) and RoBERTa-base (59.5\\%) achieve ${\\sim}$59\\% via end-to-end finetuning---19pp below feature-based classifiers (78.2\\%)---with the architecture-invariant gap ruling out model selection bias. Linear probing confirms that PLM representations cannot recover perplexity tail features (negative $R^2$), while SHAP analysis shows these features account for 88.8\\% of RF's contribution.\n\\end{itemize}",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
  "parent_tool_use_id": null,
  "session_id": "d624c1ea-2e85-4a0e-9ef8-c6452716ed01",
  "uuid": "6b076052-9d89-4c46-90cd-efae2be61ab8",
  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "We extract a 19-dimensional feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).",
    "newString": "We extract a feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\\% of full-set performance, and variance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features, placing the effective independent dimensionality at ${\\approx}$4--5.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nThis is an ordinal classification problem: depths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\nOur findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset as seed text ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each generation cycle proceeds in three steps: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$, freezing base model weights and training low-rank update matrices ($<$1\\% additional parameters; $r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$, architecture-specific targets); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages from the LoRA-finetuned model (max 512 tokens, BOS-seeded, no prompt conditioning) under the specified decoding strategy; (3)~the generated corpus becomes $\\mathcal{D}_k$ for the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model explicitly trained on the prior depth's data---not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nThere is no per-passage lineage across depths, ensuring cross-validation splits do not introduce information leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) are computed using a fixed reference model---GPT-2 base (124M)---that is independent of the three generation models.\nThe reference model computes per-token perplexity for each passage, providing a consistent measurement frame across model families.\nIn cross-model leave-one-out evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}), the reference model remains fixed even when GPT-2~XL is held out; the partial architectural overlap this introduces is discussed in Limitations.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a 19-dimensional feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments (mean, variance, skewness, kurtosis) and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles prove especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$; surprisal variance captures second-order structure not redundant with perplexity moments.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) to formalize the frontier beyond which adjacent depths become statistically indistinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\nEach pair uses an independent classifier, avoiding the decision boundary dilution that arises in multi-class settings (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$. If\\, $\\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.\n\\end{definition}\n\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ is the deepest depth $d$ such that every adjacent pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ remains discriminable at level $T$.\nThe universal quantifier ensures EDD reports the longest \\emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix rather than an isolated high-AUC pair at greater depth.\\footnote{Our AUC profiles are empirically monotonically decreasing (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), so in practice $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max\\{d'{+}1 : \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T\\}$. For non-monotonic profiles, the contiguous formulation is more conservative (see Limitations).}\nWe set $T{=}0.75$ as default; sensitivity at $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$ is reported in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas}.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold stratified cross-validation).\nWe compare against CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/generalization.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}\nSHAP TreeExplainer shows perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\\% of RF's contribution (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$ across three importance measures, $p{<}0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}).\nBoth PLM backbones correlate \\emph{negatively} with RF importance (DeBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap), with linear probing yielding negative $R^2$ for \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap})---a systematic representational gap, not backbone-specific artifacts.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from explicit tail statistics absent from PLM representations.\nThis comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}\nSHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\\% of RF's total contribution.\nThree independent importance measures---SHAP, Gini, and permutation---show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.896$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.931$, SHAP--permutation $0.839$; Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}), with \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9.\nLinear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), indicating that pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics---a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.\nThis comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question."
}
```

    "newString": "\\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}\nSHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\\% of RF's total contribution.\nThree independent importance measures---SHAP, Gini, and permutation---show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.896$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.931$, SHAP--permutation $0.839$; Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}), with \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9.\nLinear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), indicating that pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics---a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.\nThis comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether the atlas results (69.6--85.4\\%) reflect genuine statistical regularities or training artifacts, evaluating four axes: classifier choice (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and end-to-end learning (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:e2e}).\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Comparison}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} evaluates three classifier families---bagging (RF), gradient boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM)---on the same 19 features across three atlas cells spanning the full accuracy range.\nPer-cell spread is $\\leq$0.4pp, establishing a feature-determined ceiling independent of the learning algorithm.\nOn Pythia nucleus, CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020} (78.1\\%) and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} (78.2\\%) match the within-cell RF of Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} within 0.1\\,pp (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected); all feature-based methods significantly outperform CE-MLP (74.9\\%, $p<0.001$) and logistic regression (75.0\\%).\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%); a zero-shot LLM (DeepSeek V3.2) reaches only 36.2\\% (95\\% CI $[32.0, 40.4]$\\%, 500 samples).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Classifier convergence: 4-class accuracy (\\%) across three atlas cells. RF (bagging), XGBoost and LightGBM (gradient boosting) operate on the same 19 features; $\\Delta_{\\max}$: largest per-cell spread. Chance = 25\\%. RF values differ from Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} by $\\leq$0.7pp due to matched-fold evaluation.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & RF & XGB & LGBM & $\\Delta_{\\max}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc. & 78.8 & 79.0 & 78.9 & 0.2 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, $\\tau$ & 85.8 & 86.2 & 86.2 & 0.4 \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$ & 68.9 & 68.9 & 68.7 & 0.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nConvergence across five structurally different classifiers---spanning bagging, gradient boosting, ordinal regression, and ensemble detection---confirms the signal resides in the features, not the learning algorithm.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 near-perfectly identified; errors concentrate at d2/d3 in-distribution and spread across d1--d3 under transfer.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d1--d3 bal.\\ acc: Pythia 49.9\\%, OLMo 59.2\\%, GPT-2~XL 57.2\\% (mean 55.4\\%).}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy is 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance); d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline while d2 drops to 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout).\nExcluding d0, d1--d3 balanced accuracy averages 55.4\\% (chance = 33\\%).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nWe train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d0 recall $\\geq$89\\% inflates headline; d1--d3 mean: 45.9\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7pp below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%); excluding d0, d1--d3 accuracy drops to 45.9\\% (chance = 33\\%).\nd2 recall varies sharply: nucleus holdouts retain 30--54\\%, top-$k$ holdouts fall to 8--19\\%, and temperature holdouts span 3.7--39.5\\% (median 8.9\\%).\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature inverts the d1/d2 boundary (d1v2 AUC $<$ 0.5); per-strategy z-score normalization partially recovers it but leaves a residual gap (Limitations).\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{sec:gen:e2e}\n\nDeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end achieve 58.3\\% ($\\pm$4.7\\%) and 59.5\\% ($\\pm$3.2\\%) respectively---lagging the RF baseline (78.2\\%) by 19.9pp and 18.7pp.\nThe 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e_detail} shows both backbones share the same failure mode---high d0 F1 ($>$0.94) but collapsed d1/d2 performance (F1 $\\leq$ 0.42)---while RF achieves 0.596 d2 recall, a $>$2$\\times$ advantage at the hardest depth class.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class performance for end-to-end PLM classifiers vs.\\ RF baseline (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). PLM columns report F1; RF d2 column reports recall (the class with the largest PLM--RF gap). Both PLM backbones share the same failure pattern: collapsed d1/d2 scores despite adequate d0/d3.}\n\\label{tab:e2e_detail}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Acc (\\%) & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeBERTa-v3 & 58.3\\,$\\pm$\\,4.7 & .947 & .378 & .247 & .656 \\\\\nRoBERTa-base & 59.5\\,$\\pm$\\,3.2 & .958 & .416 & .261 & .647 \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 feat.) & 78.2 & --- & --- & .596$^\\dagger$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Recall; shown for the depth class with the largest PLM--RF gap.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}\nSHAP TreeExplainer shows perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\\% of RF's contribution (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$ across three importance measures, $p{<}0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}).\nBoth PLM backbones correlate \\emph{negatively} with RF importance (DeBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap), with linear probing yielding negative $R^2$ for \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap})---a systematic representational gap, not backbone-specific artifacts.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from explicit tail statistics absent from PLM representations.\nThis comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Ecological validity.}\nOn WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages), all three model-specific RF classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\\% domain shift in mean perplexity---partly an easy case, as WikiText's higher perplexity places it farther from d1--d3 in feature space.\nOn noisier web-crawl data (5{,}000 C4 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL), concentrated in template/SEO content with high n-gram repetition (Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}); deployment on heterogeneous web data requires model-specific calibration.",

```text
1	\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}
2	\label{sec:atlas}
3	
4	We present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.
5	The atlas spans $3 \times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells evaluated at depths $d \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
6	
7	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
8	\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}
9	\label{sec:atlas:id}
10	
11	Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.
12	Accuracy ranges from 69.6\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.4\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature), all exceeding the 25\% chance baseline by a wide margin.
13	Two axis effects emerge.
14	GPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (76.6--85.4\%), followed by OLMo (74.2--82.0\%) and Pythia (69.6--78.2\%).
15	Top-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.
16	The mean within-model strategy gap is 8.4~pp, comparable to the 7.1~pp mean within-strategy model gap---decoding strategy and model family contribute roughly equally to performance variance.
17	
18	\begin{table}[t]
19	\centering
20	\small
21	\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\%) across the $3 \times 3$ atlas, with d1--d3 three-class accuracy in parentheses (chance = 25\% / 33\%). Depth-0 recall exceeds 98\% in all cells.}
22	\label{tab:main_matrix}
23	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
24	\toprule
25	& Nucleus & Temp.\ $\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\
26	\midrule
27	Pythia-1.4B  & 78.2\,(71.2) & 76.3\,(70.6) & 69.6\,(58.8) \\
28	OLMo-1B     & 78.8\,(75.0) & 82.0\,(76.3) & 74.2\,(66.1) \\
29	GPT-2~XL    & 83.3\,(79.0) & 85.4\,(82.6) & 76.6\,(69.5) \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\end{tabular}
32	\end{table}
33	
34	\paragraph{Per-class recall distribution.}
35	Depth-0 recall exceeds 98\% in all 9 cells, confirming that human-written text is trivially separable from any generation depth.
36	Among generated depths, d2 consistently yields the lowest recall (46--73\% across cells), followed by d3 (58--82\%) and d1 (72--91\%).
37	For example, Pythia-1.4B nucleus achieves per-class recall of d0$\,{=}\,$99\%, d1$\,{=}\,$82\%, d2$\,{=}\,$59\%, d3$\,{=}\,$72\%.
38	The strongest d2 recall is obtained by GPT-2~XL nucleus (73\%).
39	The d2 trough reflects its position at the center of the depth gradient: d2 features overlap with both the d1 distribution above and d3 below, making it the most confusable class---consistent with the d2-vs-d3 pair being the EDD bottleneck (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
40	Full per-class recall for all cells appears in Appendix~\ref{app:per_class}.
41	
42	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
43	\subsection{Feature Universality}
44	\label{sec:atlas:features}
45	
46	Despite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.
47	The 90th-percentile perplexity (\texttt{p90\_ppl}) ranks top-2 by Gini importance in all 9 cells; surprisal variance (\texttt{var\_surprisal}) in 7/9 (8/9 under SHAP; Appendix~\ref{app:permutation}).
48	Table~\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.
49	
50	Cross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\bar{\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.
51	Among strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).
52	
53	Percentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.
54	Token-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \texttt{p90\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals generation depth.
55	
56	Feature ablation confirms this concentration: the top-5 features achieve 97.6\% of full accuracy (76.5\% vs.\ 78.4\%), with perplexity statistics alone reaching 76.6\% (vs.\ surprisal 73.2\%, other categories $<$46\%).
57	Forward selection independently confirms the dominant pair: \texttt{var\_surprisal} is selected first in 6/9 cells, \texttt{p90\_ppl} in 3/9.
58	
59	The feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.
60	At shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\ d2), \texttt{var\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\ d4, d4 vs.\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{kurtosis\_ppl}) take over.
61	This transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).
62	
63	\begin{table}[t]
64	\centering
65	\small
66	\caption{Top-5 features by Gini importance. \textbf{Bold}: top-2. \texttt{p90\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9; \texttt{var\_surprisal} 7/9 (8/9 under SHAP).}
67	\label{tab:feature_importance}
68	\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
69	\toprule
70	Cell & Top-5 features (Gini importance, ranked) \\

The $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}\n\\label{fig:feature_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{EDD Analysis}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd}\n\nWe extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.\nEach model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).\nWe train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}.\n\n\\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.\nFor Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.\nThe steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}\nThe d3-vs-d4 AUC consistently clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold across all three models (Pythia $0.784$, OLMo $0.765$, GPT-2~XL $0.750$), while d4-vs-d5 uniformly falls below $0.70$ ($0.681$--$0.684$).\nThe directional pattern is consistent, but statistical strength is stratified: bootstrap testing ($10{,}000$ resamples, $H_0{:}\\, \\mathrm{AUC} \\leq 0.75$) yields $p = 0.001$ for Pythia (significant), $p = 0.091$ for OLMo (marginal), and $p = 0.505$ for GPT-2~XL (non-significant).\nAll three models cross the $T{=}0.75$ threshold within the d3--d4 region, establishing a consistent depth-4 decay boundary with graded confidence rather than uniform statistical significance.\nThe d4-vs-d5 saturation is unambiguous across models, supporting the interpretation of d3--d4 as a \\emph{transition zone} from discriminable to indistinguishable regime.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.\nAt $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.\nThe cells losing $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the consistent bottleneck across all 9 cells.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). The d2-vs-d3 boundary is the bottleneck; top-$k$ cells drop first.}\n\\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $T{=}0.65$ & $T{=}0.70$ & $T{=}0.75$ & $T{=}0.80$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCells with $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ & 9/9 & 8/9 & 7/9 & 5/9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}\nEDD uses binary-pair classifiers to avoid \\emph{decision boundary dilution}: in Pythia nucleus, d2\\,vs.\\,d3 AUC degrades from 0.804 (4-class) to 0.689 (6-class)---a 14.3\\% relative decline despite identical data---because shared decision boundaries dilute per-pair capacity.\nBinary classifiers eliminate this confound, isolating true adjacent-pair discriminability.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}\n\nThe three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories.\nNucleus ($p{=}0.95$) produces the most consistent accuracy (78.2--83.3\\%) and serves as the reference trajectory.\nTemperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$) yields the highest accuracy in 2/3 models (GPT-2~XL 85.4\\%, OLMo 82.0\\%) because uniform logit scaling accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint---but this acceleration creates a boundary reversal risk: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features, causing cross-strategy classifiers to systematically overpredict depth (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nTop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) produces the lowest accuracy (69.6--76.6\\%) because hard vocabulary truncation removes depth-discriminative low-probability tokens, though feature direction is preserved (cross-model $\\rho = 0.811$).\nThe strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp), indicating deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy matters at least as much as the generator model.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}\n\\label{sec:atlas}\n\nWe present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.\nThe atlas spans $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells evaluated at depths $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2, 3\\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}\n\\label{sec:atlas:id}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.\nAccuracy ranges from 69.6\\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.4\\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature), all exceeding the 25\\% chance baseline by a wide margin.\nTwo axis effects emerge.\nGPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (76.6--85.4\\%), followed by OLMo (74.2--82.0\\%) and Pythia (69.6--78.2\\%).\nTop-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.\nThe mean within-model strategy gap is 8.4~pp, comparable to the 7.1~pp mean within-strategy model gap---decoding strategy and model family contribute roughly equally to performance variance.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\\%) across the $3 \\times 3$ atlas, with d1--d3 three-class accuracy in parentheses (chance = 25\\% / 33\\%). Depth-0 recall exceeds 98\\% in all cells.}\n\\label{tab:main_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& Nucleus & Temp.\\ $\\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B  & 78.2\\,(71.2) & 76.3\\,(70.6) & 69.6\\,(58.8) \\\\\nOLMo-1B     & 78.8\\,(75.0) & 82.0\\,(76.3) & 74.2\\,(66.1) \\\\\nGPT-2~XL    & 83.3\\,(79.0) & 85.4\\,(82.6) & 76.6\\,(69.5) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-class recall distribution.}\nDepth-0 recall exceeds 98\\% in all 9 cells, confirming that human-written text is trivially separable from any generation depth.\nAmong generated depths, d2 consistently yields the lowest recall (46--73\\% across cells), followed by d3 (58--82\\%) and d1 (72--91\\%).\nFor example, Pythia-1.4B nucleus achieves per-class recall of d0$\\,{=}\\,$99\\%, d1$\\,{=}\\,$82\\%, d2$\\,{=}\\,$59\\%, d3$\\,{=}\\,$72\\%.\nThe strongest d2 recall is obtained by GPT-2~XL nucleus (73\\%).\nThe d2 trough reflects its position at the center of the depth gradient: d2 features overlap with both the d1 distribution above and d3 below, making it the most confusable class---consistent with the d2-vs-d3 pair being the EDD bottleneck (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\nFull per-class recall for all cells appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:per_class}.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Feature Consistency Across the Grid}\n\\label{sec:atlas:features}\n\nDespite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.\nThe 90th-percentile perplexity (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) ranks top-2 by Gini importance in all 9 cells; surprisal variance (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) in 7/9 (8/9 under SHAP; Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.\n\nCross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\\bar{\\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.\nAmong strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\n\nPercentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.\nToken-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals generation depth.\n\nFeature ablation confirms this concentration: the top-5 features achieve 97.6\\% of full accuracy (76.5\\% vs.\\ 78.4\\%), with perplexity statistics alone reaching 76.6\\% (vs.\\ surprisal 73.2\\%, other categories $<$46\\%).\nForward selection independently confirms the dominant pair: \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} is selected first in 6/9 cells, \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} in 3/9.\n\nThe feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.\nAt shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\\ d2), \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\\ d4, d4 vs.\\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) take over.\nThis transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Top-5 features by Gini importance. \\textbf{Bold}: top-2. \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9; \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} 7/9 (8/9 under SHAP).}\n\\label{tab:feature_importance}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & Top-5 features (Gini importance, ranked) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc.       & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.15)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), var\\_ppl\\,(.08), p75\\,(.07) \\\\\nPythia, $\\tau$     & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.14)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), p75\\,(.07), m\\_surp\\,(.07) \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$    & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.16)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.12)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), var\\_ppl\\,(.07), m\\_surp\\,(.06) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, nuc.    & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.21)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.21)}, p75\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.07), m\\_ppl\\,(.06) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, $\\tau$  & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.25)}, \\textbf{p75\\,(.15)}, m\\_surp\\,(.13), var\\_surp\\,(.12), p50\\,(.05) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, top-$k$ & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.24)}, \\textbf{p75\\,(.12)}, var\\_surp\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.09), ttr\\,(.04) \\\\\nOLMo, nuc.         & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.18)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.15)}, p75\\,(.11), m\\_surp\\,(.07), ttr\\,(.06) \\\\\nOLMo, $\\tau$       & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.21)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.16)}, p75\\,(.12), m\\_surp\\,(.09), ttr\\,(.05) \\\\\nOLMo, top-$k$      & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.20)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, p75\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.07), m\\_ppl\\,(.05) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_atlas_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap. ```text
73	\end{table}
74	
75	\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}
76	\label{app:feature_overlap}
77	
78	We probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features.
79	DeBERTa attention importance and RF permutation importance exhibit a significant negative correlation (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.484$, $p{=}0.036$; top-5 Jaccard overlap$\,{=}\,0$).
80	PLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\textsc{var\_surprisal} and \textsc{p90\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing.
81	This confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\S\ref{sec:gen:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.
82	
83	\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}
84	\label{app:domain_bias}
85	
86	To assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.
87	
88	\paragraph{Methodological note.}
89	An initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\%).
90	We discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.
91	The results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.
92	
93	\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}
94	Five-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\% to 82\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\times$ above the 33.3\% chance level), confirming that depth signal is the dominant classification factor.
95	
96	\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}
97	We assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.
98	LOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\% (range 59.5--81.6\%), matching standard CV (72.0\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).
99	Per-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp, indicating that cross-group generalization is as reliable as within-group classification.
100	
101	\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}
102	We train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\%).
```

```text
138	Among C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\%), d2 (3.7\%), d3 (9.6\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\%), d2 (0.5\%), d3 (1.3\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.
139	
140	\subsection{Extended Discussion}
141	\label{app:extended_discussion}
142	
143	\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}
144	Current data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.
145	Recursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.
146	The 70--85\% in-distribution accuracy and $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest such depth-graduated filtering is feasible across the model--strategy configurations we study, though its downstream effect on training quality remains to be validated empirically.
147	Importance weighting \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.
148	
149	\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}
150	Depth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.
151	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.
152	Source attribution \cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.
153	Depth estimation characterizes \emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.
154	A layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.
155	
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	We formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that 19 statistical features achieve 70--85\% four-class accuracy ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$), with five features sufficing for 97.6\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\leq$0.4pp.
5	The dominant pair (\texttt{p90\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 7/9) generalizes across model families without retraining, making depth estimation a measurable early warning for model collapse~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}---though cross-strategy transfer remains the bottleneck (59.1\% vs.\ 66.4\%), web-crawl deployment requires model-specific calibration (C4 FPR: 4.7--22.4\%; Table~\ref{tab:calibration}), and all experiments use 1--1.5B models.
6	Future work should validate on mixed-domain corpora at larger scales and evaluate whether depth-graduated curation improves downstream training outcomes.
7	
```

      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nWe formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that 19 statistical features achieve 70--85\\% four-class accuracy ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$), with five features sufficing for 97.6\\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\\leq$0.4pp.\nThe dominant pair (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 7/9) generalizes across model families without retraining, making depth estimation a measurable early warning for model collapse~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}---though cross-strategy transfer remains the bottleneck (59.1\\% vs.\\ 66.4\\%), web-crawl deployment requires model-specific calibration (C4 FPR: 4.7--22.4\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}), and all experiments use 1--1.5B models.\nFuture work should validate on mixed-domain corpora at larger scales and evaluate whether depth-graduated curation improves downstream training outcomes.\n",
      "numLines": 7,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 7
    }
  }

    "newString": "We probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features via linear probing of [CLS] representations from DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base.\nPLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\\textsc{var\\_surprisal} and \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing, indicating these features are not linearly accessible in the pretrained representation space.\nThis confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the targeted statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Additional Results}\n\\label{app:additional}\n\n\\subsection{Top-$k$ Feature Trajectories}\n\\label{app:topk_trajectories}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:feature_trajectory} in the main text shows feature trajectories for nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) and temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$) sampling.\nTop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) trajectories follow the same monotonically decreasing trend for all features but with compressed inter-depth spacing, consistent with the signal attenuation effect discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.\nConcretely, the d0$\\to$d1 drop in \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} under top-$k$ is 78\\% (vs.\\ 87\\% for nucleus), and the d2$\\to$d3 reduction is 31\\% (vs.\\ 39\\% for nucleus).\nDespite the magnitude reduction, feature rank ordering across depths is preserved, which explains why ordinal classifiers maintain directionality even under top-$k$ conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Permutation Importance}\n\\label{app:permutation}\n\nTo verify that the Gini-based importance rankings in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features} are not an artifact of the impurity measure, we compute permutation importance (5-fold CV, 10 repeats) for all 9 atlas cells.\nThe top-2 features are stable across measures: \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in 9/9 cells under both Gini and permutation importance, and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9 (vs.\\ 7/9 for Gini, with the additional cell being OLMo top-$k$; the single SHAP exception is GPT-2~XL top-$k$, where \\textsc{p75\\_ppl} displaces \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} to rank 3).\nKendall's $W{=}0.90$ across all three methods (Gini, permutation, SHAP) indicates strong concordance ($p{<}0.005$).\nPairwise Spearman correlations are: SHAP--Gini $\\rho{=}0.93$, SHAP--permutation $\\rho{=}0.84$, Gini--permutation $\\rho{=}0.76$.\nPer-cell top-5 overlap between SHAP and permutation importance is 3--4 out of 5 features; the aggregated (mean-across-cells) top-5 sets are identical, confirming that the top features are robust to the choice of importance measure at the aggregate level while per-cell rankings exhibit model-dependent variation.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Class Recall}\n\\label{app:per_class}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:per_class_recall} reports per-class recall across all 9 atlas cells (4-class in-distribution, 5-fold CV).\nDepth~0 recall exceeds 98\\% uniformly.\nAmong generated depths, d2 is the hardest class in every cell; the deficit is most pronounced under top-$k$ sampling (46--53\\%), where vocabulary truncation removes the tail tokens that carry depth-discriminative signal.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class recall (\\%) for all 9 atlas cells. d2 is the lowest-recall class in every cell. Boldface marks the highest d2 recall.}\n\\label{tab:per_class_recall}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc.       & 99.7 & 81.9 & 58.7 & 72.3 \\\\\nPythia, $\\tau$     & 99.7 & 81.3 & 53.9 & 72.1 \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$    & 99.5 & 72.3 & 45.6 & 57.5 \\\\\nOLMo, nuc.         & 98.8 & 80.5 & 61.5 & 74.3 \\\\\nOLMo, $\\tau$       & 99.7 & 87.7 & 66.1 & 77.2 \\\\\nOLMo, top-$k$      & 99.5 & 78.5 & 51.2 & 65.6 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, nuc.     & 99.2 & 85.9 & \\textbf{73.3} & 77.5 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, $\\tau$   & 99.5 & 90.9 & 68.8 & 81.9 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, top-$k$  & 98.5 & 79.5 & 52.5 & 71.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{DCOL Iteration Details}\n\\label{app:dcol_iterations}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dcol_iterations} summarizes all six DCOL iterations.\nDeBERTa-v3-large exhibited systematic numerical instability across five configurations; RoBERTa-large resolved stability but could not close the accuracy gap with feature-based methods.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{DCOL iteration summary. All experiments use Pythia-1.4B nucleus data. RF baseline: 78.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dcol_iterations}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\nVer. & Backbone & Config & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nv1 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse (31.5\\%) \\\\\nv2 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & OOM \\\\\nv3 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & Forgetting + OOM \\\\\nv4 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse \\\\\nv5 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & NaN gradients \\\\\nv6 & RoBERTa & FT, grad clip 1.0 & Stable; 64.5\\% ($-$13.7pp) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}\n\\label{app:feature_overlap}\n\nWe probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features.\nDeBERTa attention importance and RF permutation importance exhibit a significant negative correlation (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, $p{=}0.036$; top-5 Jaccard overlap$\\,{=}\\,0$).\nPLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\\textsc{var\\_surprisal} and \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing.\nThis confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.\n\n\\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}\n\\label{app:domain_bias}\n\nTo assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological note.}\nAn initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\\%).\nWe discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.\nThe results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}\nFive-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\\% to 82\\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\\times$ above the 33.3\\% chance level), confirming that depth signal is the dominant classification factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}\nWe assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.\nLOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\\% (range 59.5--81.6\\%), matching standard CV (72.0\\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).\nPer-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp, indicating that cross-group generalization is as reliable as within-group classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}\nWe train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\\%).\nDepth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\\%---indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).\nThe depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\\times$, confirming that the feature representation encodes depth but not seed-article identity.\n\nTaken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.\n\n\\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}\n\\label{app:calibration}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+.}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Source & $n$ & d0 (\\%) & FPR (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & WikiText & 546 & 100.0 & 0.0 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nGPT-2~XL & WikiText & 546 & 99.3 & 0.7 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 77.6 & 22.4 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nOLMo-1B & WikiText & 546 & 98.5 & 1.5 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, confirming that editorial text is reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.\nOn C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\\%) vs.\\ Pythia (20.2\\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\\%).\nC4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.\nThe model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.\nAmong C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\\%), d2 (3.7\\%), d3 (9.6\\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\\%), d2 (0.5\\%), d3 (1.3\\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.\n\n\\subsection{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended_discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nRecursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nThe 70--85\\% in-distribution accuracy and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest such depth-graduated filtering is feasible across the model--strategy configurations we study, though its downstream effect on training quality remains to be validated empirically.\nImportance weighting \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.\nSource attribution \\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.\nDepth estimation characterizes \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.\nA layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n",
