the aggregated (mean-across-cells) top-5 sets are identical, confirming that the top features are robust to the choice of importance measure at the aggregate level while per-cell rankings exhibit model-dependent variation.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Class Recall}\n\\label{app:per_class}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:per_class_recall} reports per-class recall across all 9 atlas cells (4-class in-distribution, 5-fold CV).\nDepth~0 recall exceeds 98\\% uniformly.\nAmong generated depths, d2 is the hardest class in every cell; the deficit is most pronounced under top-$k$ sampling (46--53\\%), where vocabulary truncation removes the tail tokens that carry depth-discriminative signal.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class recall (\\%) for all 9 atlas cells. d2 is the lowest-recall class in every cell. Boldface marks the highest d2 recall.}\n\\label{tab:per_class_recall}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc.       & 99.7 & 81.9 & 58.7 & 72.3 \\\\\nPythia, $\\tau$     & 99.7 & 81.3 & 53.9 & 72.1 \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$    & 99.5 & 72.3 & 45.6 & 57.5 \\\\\nOLMo, nuc.         & 98.8 & 80.5 & 61.5 & 74.3 \\\\\nOLMo, $\\tau$       & 99.7 & 87.7 & 66.1 & 77.2 \\\\\nOLMo, top-$k$      & 99.5 & 78.5 & 51.2 & 65.6 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, nuc.     & 99.2 & 85.9 & \\textbf{73.3} & 77.5 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, $\\tau$   & 99.5 & 90.9 & 68.8 & 81.9 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, top-$k$  & 98.5 & 79.5 & 52.5 & 71.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{DCOL Iteration Details}\n\\label{app:dcol_iterations}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dcol_iterations} summarizes all six DCOL iterations.\nDeBERTa-v3-large exhibited systematic numerical instability across five configurations; RoBERTa-large resolved stability but could not close the accuracy gap with feature-based methods.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{DCOL iteration summary. All experiments use Pythia-1.4B nucleus data. RF baseline: 78.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dcol_iterations}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\nVer. & Backbone & Config & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nv1 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse (31.5\\%) \\\\\nv2 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & OOM \\\\\nv3 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & Forgetting + OOM \\\\\nv4 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse \\\\\nv5 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & NaN gradients \\\\\nv6 & RoBERTa & FT, grad clip 1.0 & Stable; 64.5\\% ($-$13.7pp) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}\n\\label{app:feature_overlap}\n\nWe probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features via linear probing of [CLS] representations from DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base.\nPLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\\textsc{var\\_surprisal} and \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing, indicating these features are not linearly accessible in the pretrained representation space.\nThis confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the targeted statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.\n\n\\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}\n\\label{app:domain_bias}\n\nTo assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological note.}\nAn initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\\%).\nWe discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.\nThe results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}\nFive-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\\% to 82\\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\\times$ above the 33.3\\% chance level), confirming that depth signal is the dominant classification factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}\nWe assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.\nLOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\\% (range 59.5--81.6\\%), matching standard CV (72.0\\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).\nPer-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp, indicating that cross-group generalization is as reliable as within-group classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}\nWe train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\\%).\nDepth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\\%---indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).\nThe depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\\times$, confirming that the feature representation encodes depth but not seed-article identity.\n\nTaken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.\n\n\\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}\n\\label{app:calibration}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+.}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Source & $n$ & d0 (\\%) & FPR (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & WikiText & 546 & 100.0 & 0.0 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nGPT-2~XL & WikiText & 546 & 99.3 & 0.7 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 77.6 & 22.4 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nOLMo-1B & WikiText & 546 & 98.5 & 1.5 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, confirming that editorial text is reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.\nOn C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\\%) vs.\\ Pythia (20.2\\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\\%).\nC4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.\nThe model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.\nAmong C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\\%), d2 (3.7\\%), d3 (9.6\\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\\%), d2 (0.5\\%), d3 (1.3\\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.\n\n\\subsection{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended_discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nRecursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nThe 70--85\\% in-distribution accuracy and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest such depth-graduated filtering is feasible across the model--strategy configurations we study, though its downstream effect on training quality remains to be validated empirically.\nImportance weighting \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.\nSource attribution \\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.\nDepth estimation characterizes \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.\nA layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n",

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
  "old_string": "We formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that 19 statistical features achieve 70--85\\% four-class accuracy ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$), with five features sufficing for 97.6\\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\\leq$0.4pp.\nThe dominant pair (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 7/9) generalizes across model families without retraining, making depth estimation a measurable early warning for model collapse~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}---though cross-strategy transfer remains the bottleneck (59.1\\% vs.\\ 66.4\\%), web-crawl deployment requires model-specific calibration (C4 FPR: 4.7--22.4\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}), and all experiments use 1--1.5B models.\nFuture work should validate on mixed-domain corpora at larger scales and evaluate whether depth-graduated curation improves downstream training outcomes.",
  "new_string": "We formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that a compact feature set dominated by perplexity tail statistics achieves 70--85\\% four-class accuracy ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$), with five features sufficing for 97.6\\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\\leq$0.4pp.\nThe dominant pair (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9 under SHAP) generalizes across model families without retraining, making depth estimation a measurable early warning for model collapse~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}.\n\nCross-strategy transfer remains the primary bottleneck (59.1\\% vs.\\ 66.4\\% cross-model), with the temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion making single-classifier deployment across unknown decoding strategies infeasible.\nA two-stage pipeline---first identifying the decoding strategy, then applying a strategy-conditioned depth classifier---could address this, though automating strategy identification from text alone requires independent validation.\nDepth-graduated data curation is conceptually enabled by our method, but the d1/d2 boundary reversal and 26.8--52.6\\% d2 recall in transfer settings preclude direct deployment without strategy metadata or a reliable strategy-identification front-end.\nReal-world documents may also interleave passages at mixed recursive depths within a single document; our passage-level classifier assumes uniform depth, and extending to segment-level estimation introduces a granularity--statistical-power tradeoff that remains unexplored.\nWeb-crawl deployment requires model-specific calibration (C4 FPR: 4.7--22.4\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}), and all experiments use 1--1.5B models.\nFuture work should validate on mixed-domain corpora at larger scales and evaluate whether depth-graduated curation, with appropriate strategy conditioning, improves downstream training outcomes."
}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WcRahEaUiQULnAUkxc7K6Y`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	We formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that a compact feature set dominated by perplexity tail statistics achieves 70--85\% four-class accuracy ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$), with five features sufficing for 97.6\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\leq$0.4pp.
5	The dominant pair (\texttt{p90\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 8/9 under SHAP) generalizes across model families without retraining, making depth estimation a measurable early warning for model collapse~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}.
6	
7	Cross-strategy transfer remains the primary bottleneck (59.1\% vs.\ 66.4\% cross-model), with the temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion making single-classifier deployment across unknown decoding strategies infeasible.
8	A two-stage pipeline---first identifying the decoding strategy, then applying a strategy-conditioned depth classifier---could address this, though automating strategy identification from text alone requires independent validation.
9	Depth-graduated data curation is conceptually enabled by our method, but the d1/d2 boundary reversal and 26.8--52.6\% d2 recall in transfer settings preclude direct deployment without strategy metadata or a reliable strategy-identification front-end.
10	Real-world documents may also interleave passages at mixed recursive depths within a single document; our passage-level classifier assumes uniform depth, and extending to segment-level estimation introduces a granularity--statistical-power tradeoff that remains unexplored.
11	Web-crawl deployment requires model-specific calibration (C4 FPR: 4.7--22.4\%; Table~\ref{tab:calibration}), and all experiments use 1--1.5B models.
12	Future work should validate on mixed-domain corpora at larger scales and evaluate whether depth-graduated curation, with appropriate strategy conditioning, improves downstream training outcomes.
13	
```

```json
  "uuid": "4af5f8e1-17a1-4d51-a0cd-036aea575c36",
  "tool_use_result": {
    "type": "text",
    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nWe formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that a compact feature set dominated by perplexity tail statistics achieves 70--85\\% four-class accuracy ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$), with five features sufficing for 97.6\\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\\leq$0.4pp.\nThe dominant pair (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9 under SHAP) generalizes across model families without retraining, making depth estimation a measurable early warning for model collapse~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}.\n\nCross-strategy transfer remains the primary bottleneck (59.1\\% vs.\\ 66.4\\% cross-model), with the temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion making single-classifier deployment across unknown decoding strategies infeasible.\nA two-stage pipeline---first identifying the decoding strategy, then applying a strategy-conditioned depth classifier---could address this, though automating strategy identification from text alone requires independent validation.\nDepth-graduated data curation is conceptually enabled by our method, but the d1/d2 boundary reversal and 26.8--52.6\\% d2 recall in transfer settings preclude direct deployment without strategy metadata or a reliable strategy-identification front-end.\nReal-world documents may also interleave passages at mixed recursive depths within a single document; our passage-level classifier assumes uniform depth, and extending to segment-level estimation introduces a granularity--statistical-power tradeoff that remains unexplored.\nWeb-crawl deployment requires model-specific calibration (C4 FPR: 4.7--22.4\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}), and all experiments use 1--1.5B models.\nFuture work should validate on mixed-domain corpora at larger scales and evaluate whether depth-graduated curation, with appropriate strategy conditioning, improves downstream training outcomes.\n",
      "numLines": 13,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 13
    }
  }
}
```

```text
115	\end{table}
116	
117	\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}
118	SHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\% of RF's total contribution.
119	Three independent importance measures---SHAP, Gini, and permutation---show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.896$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.931$, SHAP--permutation $0.839$; Appendix~\ref{app:permutation}), with \texttt{p90\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 8/9.
120	Linear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \texttt{var\_surprisal} and \texttt{p90\_ppl} (Appendix~\ref{app:feature_overlap}), indicating that pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics---a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.
121	
122	DCOL \cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\% (Appendix~\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.
123	This comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.
124	
125	\paragraph{Ecological validity.}
126	On WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages), all three model-specific RF classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\% domain shift in mean perplexity---partly an easy case, as WikiText's higher perplexity places it farther from d1--d3 in feature space.
127	On noisier web-crawl data (5{,}000 C4 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\% (OLMo) to 22.4\% (GPT-2~XL), concentrated in template/SEO content with high n-gram repetition (Table~\ref{tab:calibration}); deployment on heterogeneous web data requires model-specific calibration.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014DmP5Z8GRiNMU8G2Q6vMsr`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	As large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\to$data$\to$model loop arises---yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.
3	We formalize \emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.
4	From each passage we extract a compact statistical fingerprint---19 candidate features dominated by perplexity tail statistics, with the top 5 achieving 97.6\% of full-set performance---computed against a fixed reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
5	A Random Forest classifier achieves 70--85\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\%; 58.8--82.6\% when excluding the near-perfectly separable d0 class; a zero-shot LLM baseline reaches only 36.2\%), with 90th-percentile perplexity and surprisal variance emerging as consistent depth markers across the studied $3 \times 3$ grid (top-2 importance in 9/9 and 7/9 cells, respectively).
6	Our \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas} reveals that decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories rather than merely attenuating them, and we define the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for extended recursion chains.
7	Cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\%; temperature sampling induces a d1/d2 AUC inversion that we decompose into a correctable scale component and a residual structural component.
8	Applied to WikiText-103 (546 passages), all three model-specific classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\% domain shift in mean perplexity; on C4 web-crawl text, false positive rates range from 4.7\% to 22.4\%, concentrated in template content.
9	\end{abstract}
```
